Dear Secretary Reese: 
Pursuant to Section 205 of the Federal Power Act (“FPA”)1 and sections 35.3 and 35.13 of 
the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission’s (“Commission” or “FERC”) regulations,2 and in 
compliance with the Commission’s June 18, 2026, order to show cause (“OSC Order”) issued  to 
the California Independent System Operator Corporation (“CAISO”) and the CAISO Participating 
Transmission Owners (“PTOs”),3 DCR Transmission, L.L.C. (“DCRT”) hereby submits proposed 
revisions to its Transmission Owner Tariff (“TO Tariff”)4 to address issues regarding large loads 
identified by the Commission’s OSC Order (“Large Loads”).5  As explained herein, DCRT’s 
proposed TO Tariff revisions are just and reasonable and comply with the OSC Order.  DCRT 
respectfully requests that the Commission accept the proposed Tariff revisions, to be effective 
February 15, 2027.6

I. 
BACKGROUND 
A. 
DCRT 
DCRT owns and operates the 125-mile, 500 kilovolt Ten West Link Transmission Line 
(“Ten West Link”), which runs from Arizona Public Service Company’s (“APS”) Delaney 
Substation, in Maricopa County, Arizona, to Southern California Edison Company’s (“SCE”) 
Colorado River Substation, in Riverside County, California.  DCRT has transferred operational 
control of Ten West Link to the CAISO pursuant to the CAISO’s Transmission Control Agreement 
and is a PTO subject to that agreement.7 DCRT also maintains its own TO Tariff.8   
Ten West Link entered commercial operation on June 12, 2024, and is currently one of the 
few transmission interconnections between Arizona and Southern California.  Approximately 100 
miles of Ten West Link are in Arizona. 
B. 
Commission’s Show Cause Order  
On June 18, 2026, the Commission issued orders to each of the six Independent System 
Operators (“ISOs”) and Regional Transmission Organizations (“RTOs”) over which it exercises 
jurisdiction and the PTOs within those ISOs and RTOs.9  These orders addressed potential 
deficiencies identified by the Commission regarding the integration of Large Loads onto the bulk 
power system.10  One such order was directed at the CAISO and the CAISO PTOs.  DCRT is one 
such PTO within the CAISO and, therefore, subject to the Commission’s OSC Order.11  
In the OSC Order, the Commission directed the CAISO and the CAISO PTOs, as 
applicable, to address the requirements for Large Load interconnections, either through proposed 
tariff revisions or an explanation for why such revisions to existing tariffs are not needed.12  The 
Commission requested that the CAISO address the following:  
• Rates, terms, and conditions of service that apply to interconnection customers serving co-
located Large Load to address: 
• The scope of the analyses the CAISO performs as part of its existing study process 
to evaluate the impacts of co-location arrangements, which may need to account 
for any potential dynamic impacts of co-located Large Loads and any potential 
 
facilitates consistency between DCRT’s proposed Large Load Tariff revisions and related tariff revisions submitted 
by the CAISO.

impacts of requests to modify an existing generating facility’s interconnection 
service level to serve co-located Large Loads on transmission system reliability; 
• The potential that Eligible Customers,13 on behalf of Large Load, may not be 
required to pay for wholesale transmission services that they receive, as required 
by the cost causation principle; 
• Netting Behind-the-Meter Generation (“BTMG”) against its Large Load for 
purposes of calculating Regional Access Charges; 
• Generator interconnection service(s) that reflects the operational dynamics of 
serving either an electrically proximate Large Load or a co-located load that has a 
high peak load; and  
• Transmission services that reflect that an Eligible Customer taking transmission 
service on behalf of Large Load is willing and able to limit its energy withdrawals 
from the transmission system under certain conditions, such as:  
o An interim non-firm network transmission service while Network Upgrades 
are being constructed (i.e., interim Network Integration Transmission

o Permanent firm and non-firm contract demand transmission services

• 
A definition of Large Load sufficient to capture loads that present the unique 
challenges described in the OSC Order; 
• 
The application process for requesting transmission service to Eligible Customers 
on behalf of Large Loads; 
• 
Procedures for studying the provision of transmission service to Large Loads that: 
o Identify which party will be responsible for conducting such studies; 
o Consider the unique characteristics and impacts of Large Loads on the 
transmission system, including Large Loads that are able to limit their 
energy withdrawals under certain conditions, which may thereby lessen or 
potentially avoid the need for certain Network Upgrades;

o Deter speculative or duplicative requests for transmission service by 
Eligible Customers on behalf of Large Loads; 
o Are timely completed within 60–90 days; 
o Require the evaluation of Alternative Transmission Technologies (“ATTs”) 
in studies for the provision of transmission service, using models that are 
capable of evaluating the transmission system to accurately account for 
ATTs, in all instances, without the need for a request from the Eligible

costs or a faster timeline for accommodating the transmission service 
request;  
•

necessary to ensure reliable operation of the transmission system;  
• 
Pro forma provisions in transmission service agreements, or other applicable 
agreements, to memorialize the procedures listed above; 
• 
Mechanisms to mitigate the risk of cost-shifting among transmission customers, 
which may result in unjust and unreasonable rates for transmission; 
• 
Provision of data on the CAISO’s website in a single location, and in an easily 
accessible format that is searchable and allows users to filter the data, on 
transmission service provided to Eligible Customers on behalf of Large Loads, for 
Network Upgrades evaluated in the local transmission planning process that are 
needed to accommodate transmission service to Eligible Customers on behalf of 
those Large Loads, and the costs for those Network Upgrades.15 
The Commission recommended that the CAISO and the CAISO PTOs coordinate amongst 
each other in the development of the relevant Tariff provisions for compliance with the OSC 
Order’s directives.16

C. 
DRCT’s Understanding of the CAISO’s Position 
Based on the OSC Order, DCRT participated in the CAISO’s stakeholder process for the 
CAISO PTOs to coordinate the CAISO’s and the CAISO PTO’s compliance filing in response to 
the OSC Order.  As recommended by the Commission,17 the CAISO and the CAISO PTOs 
coordinated the development of their respective tariff revisions.18 The CAISO and the CAISO 
PTOs held weekly coordination meetings where the CAISO PTOs and the CAISO could apply 
shared technical expertise to the Commission’s identified issues and revise internal draft proposals 
before dissemination to the public for comments via the CAISO’s stakeholder process.  The 
CAISO PTOs also held regular meetings amongst themselves to apply shared expertise and jointly 
address CAISO PTO-specific issues where possible.  The CAISO utilized its existing stakeholder 
processes to develop its proposed tariff revisions. 
Through participation in the CAISO’s coordination process and standard stakeholder 
process, DCRT understands the CAISO’s position with respect to the OSC Order to be that: 
• 
The CAISO will define Large Loads as those that are 50 MW or greater and 
interconnected with the CAISO Controlled Grid, which definition DCRT will 
adopt; 
• 
The CAISO PTOs will address load interconnection procedures (including for 
Large Loads), including for co-located Large Loads, as is the current practice 
within the CAISO; 
• 
The CAISO will address interconnection of generators that export power to the 
CAISO Controlled Grid, including for co-located generation, as is the current 
practice within the CAISO; 
• 
The CAISO PTOs will address the risk of cost-shifting among Eligible Customers; 
• 
The CAISO and the CAISO PTOs will jointly address ongoing operational 
requirements for Large Loads; 
• 
The CAISO and the CAISO PTOs will jointly address the provision of interim non-
firm interconnection service through the Flexible Interim Load Interconnection 
(“FILI”) Service; 
• 
Large Load developers have not expressed interest in permanent non-firm 
transmission service to any of the CAISO PTOs; therefore, such a transmission 
service is not warranted at this time; 
 
17 See OSC Order, at P 37.

following link to the CAISO internet website: https://stakeholdercenter.caiso.com/StakeholderInitiatives/Large-loads.

• 
The CAISO and the CAISO PTOs will jointly address provision of data on the 
CAISO’s website in a single location, and in an easily accessible format that is 
searchable and allows users to filter the data, on transmission service provided to 
Eligible Customers on behalf of Large Loads, for Network Upgrades evaluated in 
the local transmission planning process that are needed to accommodate 
transmission service to Eligible Customers on behalf of those Large Loads, and the 
costs for those Network Upgrades; and  
• 
Specific study procedures for generator interconnection regarding electrically 
proximate Large Loads are not necessary because the CAISO is not capacity-
constrained. 
The CAISO’s and the CAISO PTO’s coordination processes were dynamic, with many 
opinions and perspectives shared and vigorous debates among the parties.  DCRT understands the 
opinions of the other parties but tailored its proposed tariff revisions to its unique situation as a 
single-asset PTO whose transmission line traverses the California-Arizona border.  DCRT’s filing 
reflects DCRT’s understanding of how the CAISO envisions the split of responsibilities between 
the CAISO and the CAISO PTOs for purposes of Large Load interconnection based on the OSC 
Order.  
From DCRT’s understanding, the CAISO will not revise its offering of transmission 
products for Large Load interconnection.19  DCRT, as a PTO, cannot independently offer specific 
transmission products under the DCRT TO Tariff that may be inconsistent with services provided 
by the CAISO. Therefore, DCRT has not proposed additional or revised transmission services in 
this filing.20   
II. 
DCRT’S PROPOSED TARIFF REVISIONS ARE JUST AND REASONABLE 
AND COMPLY WITH THE DIRECTIVES OF THE OSC ORDER 
A. 
The Interconnection Study Process Proposed in DCRT’s TO Tariff, Appendix IV 
and Large Load Interconnection Agreement Satisfies the Directives of the OSC 
Order 
In the OSC Order, the Commission preliminarily found that the CAISO Tariff and/or the 
TO Tariffs appear to be unjust and unreasonable because they lack sufficiently clear and consistent 
provisions establishing the application process and study procedures that will be used to evaluate 
the provision of transmission service to Eligible Customers on behalf of Large Loads.21  The 
Commission explained its concern that, absent such provisions, existing processes may not be

customers, which would require redesigning transmission and interconnection service entirely. The CAISO does not 
believe such a redesign is warranted.”).

November 16, 2026

described with the clarity and specificity necessary to mitigate the operational risks of serving 
Large Loads, avoid disputes regarding how Large Load-related studies are conducted, deter 
speculative or duplicative requests for transmission service, and help avoid excessive and 
unnecessary Network Upgrades.22   
DCRT proposes to add a new Appendix IV to its TO Tariff to directly respond to each of 
these concerns, as described below.23 The new Appendix IV includes a new Large Load 
Interconnection Process (“LLIP”) which details the interconnection process required for Large 
Loads to interconnect to Ten West Link for purposes of taking transmission service over the 
CAISO system, and includes a new pro forma Large Load Interconnection Agreement (“LLIA”). 
The combination of DCRT’s new LLIP and LLIA provide the level of transparency and detail 
sought in the OSC Order for the Large Loads, so transmission-related services are provided in a 
just, reasonable and not unduly discriminatory manner.  
1.
Clear Assignment of Study Responsibility
In the OSC Order, the Commission expressed concern that the CAISO Tariff and/or the 
TO Tariffs may not clearly specify which entity will be responsible for studying the provision of 
transmission service to Eligible Customers on behalf of Large Loads.24  DCRT will not be 
administrating transmission service to Large Load Interconnection Customers. The CAISO will 
be providing that service. DCRT will be providing interconnection to Large Loads. DCRT’s 
proposed Tariff revisions clearly assign responsibilities for the LLIP and state that DCRT will 
process Large Load interconnections.  DCRT TO Tariff, proposed Appendix IV, Section 2 
obligates DCRT to perform the two studies that frame Large Load interconnections: the Large 
Load System Impact Study, and the Large Load Facilities Study.25  By memorializing this 
obligation in its TO Tariff, DCRT provides Large Load Interconnection Customers with certainty 
as to which entity is accountable for each phase of the Large Load study process.  
2.
Rolling Application Process
In the OSC Order, the Commission directed the CAISO and the PTOs to explain whether 
their tariffs remain just and reasonable without, among other things, an application process that 
accepts Eligible Customers’ applications for transmission service on behalf of Large Loads on a 
rolling basis.26  DCRT’s proposed Tariff revisions establish a rolling application process for Large 
Load interconnections.  DCRT TO Tariff, new Appendix IV, Section 1.2 provides that DCRT will 
22 See OSC Order, at P 49.

among other things, arrangements that provide for station service directly from the local franchised public 
utility.  Appendix A also provides that DCRT will coordinate with the Large Load Interconnection Customer to 
facilitate any necessary pseudo-tie or similar arrangement for the delivery of power into the CAISO BAA.

time-stamp each application upon receipt to establish study priority.27  This approach permits 
Large Load Interconnection Customers to submit applications whenever their projects are ready, 
rather than awaiting a periodic window, while providing an objective, transparent, and non-
discriminatory basis for determining the order in which requests will be studied. 
3. 
Non-Refundable Application and Study Fees 
In the OSC Order, the Commission identified a non-refundable application fee as one tool 
to deter duplicative or speculative requests for transmission service on behalf of Large Loads.28  
DCRT’s proposed Tariff revisions include meaningful non-refundable fees for Large Load 
interconnections.  DCRT TO Tariff, proposed Appendix IV, Section 1.4.2 provides for a non-
refundable application fee of two-hundred-thousand dollars ($200,000) that also covers the Large 
Load System Impact Study fees, and Section 2.3.2 establishes a non-refundable study fee of two-
hundred-thousand dollars ($200,000) for the Large Load Facilities Study.  For both studies, the 
Large Load Interconnection Customer is obligated to reimburse DCRT for any study costs in 
excess of the default fee.29  Moreover, by requiring the Large Load Interconnection Customer to 
bear study costs in excess of the fee, the proposed revisions ensure that the cost of studying a Large 
Load request is borne by the requesting party rather than by DCRT’s other customers. 
4. 
Readiness Requirements  
In the OSC Order, the Commission preliminarily found that sufficient readiness 
requirements that escalate at distinct phases of the study process (e.g., meaningful milestones 
and/or financial commitments) may be necessary to deter duplicative or speculative requests for 
transmission service.30  DCRT’s proposed Tariff revisions establish readiness requirements that 
escalate as an application progresses through the study process for Large Load interconnections.  
DCRT TO Tariff, proposed Appendix IV provides several readiness tools, including: (1) evidence 
of site control; (2) an attestation by an officer of the Large Load Interconnection Customer; (3) a 
deadline by which the Large Load Interconnection Customer must elect to proceed following the 
completion of each study phase, failing which the application will be deemed withdrawn; and (4) 
a limit of one amendment to the application.31   
DCRT also has proposed requiring detailed dynamic models as part of the Large Load 
Interconnection Customer’s initial application and an initial sufficiency review of applications.32 
Such models require meaningful effort and expertise to create, helping filter out speculative 
requests.  Lastly, DCRT is proposing that Large Load Interconnection Customers be required to

post financial security at three specific milestones or the application will be withdrawn.  The details 
of these security postings are addressed in proposed Appendix IV, Attachment 4.  
Together, these readiness requirements ensure that only those interconnection requests that 
are genuinely viable, and that the Large Load Interconnection Customer is prepared to pursue, 
continue to consume study resources.  This protects the integrity of the study queue for all Large 
Load Interconnection Customers and reduces the risk that Network Upgrades are planned for Large 
Loads that never materialize. 
5. 
Evaluation of ATTs 
In the OSC Order, the Commission preliminarily found that the CAISO Tariff and/or the 
TO Tariffs appear to be unjust and unreasonable because they lack clear and consistent provisions 
requiring the evaluation of ATTs as potential solutions to accommodate an Eligible Customer’s 
request for transmission service on behalf of a Large Load.33 Specifically, the Commission directed 
the CAISO and the CAISO PTOs to explain whether their tariffs remain just and reasonable 
without provisions that: (1) require the evaluation of ATTs in studies for the provision of 
transmission service, using models that are capable of evaluating the transmission system to 
accurately account for advanced transmission technologies, in all instances, without the need for a 
request from the Eligible Customer seeking transmission service on behalf of a Large Load; and 
(2) if traditional Network Upgrades are selected instead of ATTs, require inclusion in the study 
report to the Eligible Customer of a sufficiently clear demonstration as to why ATTs are not 
feasible (i.e., would not resolve reliability violations identified or meet the relevant planning 
criteria) or would not result in lower costs or a faster timeline for accommodating the transmission 
service request.34  In so doing, the Commission explained that ATTs can add capacity on existing 
transmission lines quickly, often within one to three years, which may be more preferable for Large 
Loads.35 
For purposes of the OSC Order, the Commission defined ATTs to include static 
synchronous compensators, static VAR compensators, advanced power flow control devices, 
transmission switching, synchronous condensers, voltage source converters, advanced conductors, 
tower lifting, and dynamic line ratings, and noted that the Commission has also referred to these 
technologies as Grid Enhancing Technologies.36  DCRT’s proposed Tariff revisions address the 
Commission’s directives regarding the evaluation of ATTs.   DCRT’s proposed Tariff revisions 
include study procedures that will evaluate whether such technologies can resolve identified 
reliability needs when evaluating the transmission upgrades necessary to interconnect a new Large 
Load, and set forth the decision-making process for determining when to utilize an ATT rather 
than a traditional transmission upgrade. 
 
33 See OSC Order, at P 60. 
34 See OSC Order, at PP 61, 126. 
35 See OSC Order, at P 60.  
36 See OSC Order, at P 60 n.154.

Importantly, the Commission emphasized that it is not mandating the use of ATTs.37 
Rather, the Commission’s directive is that ATTs be evaluated and, where they are not selected, 
that the basis for that determination be transparently explained to the Eligible Customer.38  
To comply with the Commission’s directives regarding ATTs, DCRT’s proposed Tariff 
revisions explicitly state that ATTs, as defined in the OSC Order, will be considered, as applicable, 
during the Large Load System Impact Study and the Large Load Facilities Study when evaluating 
necessary transmission upgrades.39  DCRT’s revisions also explain the decision-making process 
for when to utilize an ATT versus a traditional transmission upgrade to ensure reliability while 
interconnecting a new Large Load.40  Specifically, if a traditional Network Upgrade solution is 
selected instead of ATTs, the study report will explain why ATTs are not feasible, ensuring that 
ATTs are appropriately considered during the study process.41 DCRT’s proposal will ensure that 
ATTs receive a meaningful evaluation in every Large Load study, while preserving DCRT’s 
engineering judgment to select the solution that will reliably and cost-effectively accommodate 
the requested service on DCRT’s single-line system. 
6. 
Study Timelines  
The OSC Order preliminarily found that the CAISO’s Tariff and/or the TO Tariffs appear 
to be unjust and unreasonable because they lack provisions that address, with sufficient clarity and 
consistency, how the CAISO and/or the CAISO PTOs will timely study (i.e., within 60–90 days 
of receiving the request) the provision of transmission service to Eligible Customers on behalf of 
Large Loads.42  DCRT’s proposed Tariff revisions address the OSC Order’s directives for purposes 
of Large Load interconnection study timelines.   
DCRT’s proposed Tariff revisions meet the 90-day study deadline directed by the OSC 
Order by specifying the following benchmarks for the application and study process: (1) 
sufficiency review of the initial application to take five (5) business days, averaging 7 calendar 
days; (2) if deficiencies are identified, three (3) business days for the Large Load Interconnection 
Customer to cure the deficiencies; (3) five (5) calendar days to review any re-submitted 
application; (4) seven (7) calendar days for DCRT to execute the Large Load System Impact Study 
Agreement and conduct an initial scoping meeting with the Large Load Interconnection Customer; 
(5) concurrently with step 4, thirty (30) days to conduct the Large Load System Impact Study and 
submit a study report; (6) seven (7) days for the Large Load Interconnection Customer to review 
the Large Load System Impact Study report and execute a Large Load Facilities Study Agreement; 
and (7) thirty (30) days for DCRT to conduct the Large Load Facilities Study and submit a report.43

In total, these steps will average roughly 85 calendar days from start to finish, meeting the OSC

B. 
Overview of DCRT’s Pro Forma Large Load Interconnection Agreement 
DCRT TO Tariff, proposed Appendix IV, Attachment 3, includes DCRT’s proposed pro 
forma LLIA, generally modeled on the CAISO’s pro forma Large Generator Interconnection 
Agreement (“LGIA”), set forth in the CAISO Tariff, Appendix EE, which has been accepted by 
the Commission.44  In developing its proposed  LLIA, DCRT began with the CAISO LGIA and 
removed those provisions that are specific to the interconnection and operation of generating 
facilities and have no application to a Large Load—for example, provisions addressing power 
system stabilizers, generator reactive power and primary frequency response obligations (replaced 
with a composite power factor requirement for the Large Load Facility), the Merchant Option, data 
requirements for Variable Energy Resources, and the interconnection requirements for 
Asynchronous Generating Facilities.45 At the same time, DCRT retained the interconnection and 
Network Upgrade concepts of the CAISO pro forma LGIA that apply equally to the 
interconnection of a Large Load, including the provisions governing Interconnection Facilities, 
Network Upgrades, and Distribution Upgrades; the engineering, procurement, and construction of 
such facilities; the Scoping Meeting; coordination with, and agreements involving, Affected 
Systems; milestones and financial security; and the provision of construction status reports to the 
CAISO.  DCRT took this approach deliberately, so that the terminology, structure, and allocation 
of responsibilities among the CAISO, DCRT, and the Large Load Interconnection Customer in the 
LLIA remain consistent with those in the Commission-accepted CAISO LGIA.  Doing so 
promotes clarity and consistency across the CAISO footprint, allows Large Load Interconnection 
Customers, the CAISO, and other market participants to rely on familiar and well-understood 
contractual terms, and minimizes the risk of disputes regarding the meaning of defined terms—
consistent with the Commission’s emphasis in the OSC Order on clear and consistent tariff 
provisions.46  The DCRT proposed LLIA is different from the CAISO pro forma LGIA in certain 
provisions that reflect DCRT’s unique circumstances, most notably that DCRT’s single 
transmission line interconnects the CAISO Controlled Grid with the APS franchised service 
territory in Arizona.  
Beyond the study, application, cost-responsibility, and FILI provisions addressed 
elsewhere in this transmittal letter and in the DCRT TO Tariff, the LLIA contains a number of key 
regulatory provisions, each carried over from, and consistent with, the corresponding provisions 
of the Commission-accepted CAISO LGIA.  First, the proposed DCRT LLIA becomes effective 
upon execution by all Parties subject to acceptance by the Commission or, if filed unexecuted, on 
the date specified by the Commission, and DCRT will file the LLIA and any amendments with the

is available in FERC eTariff.

Commission as required.47  Second, and importantly in light of the CAISO’s unique transmission 
service framework, the LLIA makes clear that its execution does not constitute a request for, or 
the provision of, any transmission service under the CAISO Tariff and does not convey any right 
to receive electricity at any specific point of delivery; transmission service remains available only 
through the CAISO’s existing processes.48  Third, the proposed DCRT LLIA requires each Party 
to perform its obligations in accordance with Applicable Laws and Regulations, Applicable 
Reliability Standards, and Good Utility Practice, and to comply with the Emergency Condition 
procedures of the CAISO, the North American Electric Reliability Corporation (“NERC”), and the 
applicable Regional Reliability Council, while preserving the authority of DCRT to take actions 
necessary to preserve the reliability of the DCRT Transmission System during an Emergency 
Condition, including reduction or disconnection of the interconnection.49  Fourth, the proposed 
DCRT LLIA preserves the Parties’ respective rights to make unilateral filings under FPA sections 
205 and 206.50  Collectively, these provisions ensure that the rights and obligations of the Parties 
under the LLIA are transparent, enforceable, and subject to the Commission’s continuing 
oversight.  
In addition to these regulatory provisions, DCRT’s proposed LLIA contains the standard 
commercial provisions found in the Commission-accepted CAISO LGIA, adapted only as 
necessary to apply to a Large Load rather than a generating facility.  These include: (1) invoicing, 
payment, and billing-dispute procedures, including the requirement that a Party continue 
performance during a billing dispute so long as undisputed amounts are paid and disputed amounts 
are paid or placed in escrow, with interest calculated under the Commission’s regulations;51 (2) 
termination procedures and the Large Load Interconnection Customer’s responsibility for 
termination costs, including costs incurred or irrevocably committed for the construction of 
DCRT’s Interconnection Facilities and Network Upgrades;52 (3) default and cure provisions 
affording a Breaching Party thirty (30) Calendar Days, or up to ninety (90) Calendar Days for a 
breach not capable of earlier cure, to remedy a Breach before the non-breaching Party may 
terminate;53 (4) force majeure;54 (5) mutual indemnification, a mutual waiver of special, indirect,

As proposed in DCRT’s LLIP, DCRT will evaluate the specific operating requirements for

as a party to the extent there is co-location and BTMG facilities that inject power to the CAISO-
controlled grid and that injection of power must be coordinated with the relevant Large Load

C. 
DCRT’s Large Load Interconnection Process is Designed to Operate in Close 
Coordination with the CAISO, Including with Respect to Co-Located 
Generation and BTMG 
DCRT emphasizes that the central focus of its proposed TO Tariff revisions is to establish 
a just and reasonable, transparent, and clearly delineated process through which a Large Load 
Interconnection Customer may interconnect a Large Load to DCRT’s facilities, consistent with the 
requirements of the OSC Order.  DCRT recognizes, however, that the interconnection of a Large 
Load to its system does not occur in isolation.  Because DCRT’s facilities are under the operational 
control of the CAISO, and because the CAISO remains responsible for the interconnection of 
generating facilities—including co-located generation—to the CAISO Controlled Grid, DCRT’s 
proposed LLIP have been designed to operate in close coordination with the CAISO to ensure the 
reliability of both DCRT’s facilities and the CAISO-controlled transmission system.65  
Accordingly, upon Commission acceptance of DCRT’s LLIP, DCRT will coordinate and 
collaborate with the CAISO regarding both the interconnection of Large Loads and, as explained 
below, the co-location of generation and BTMG with such Large Loads, as further memorialized 
in an Appendix H to the LLIA. To that end, DCRT’s proposed LLIA establishes several specific 
mechanisms through which DCRT will keep the CAISO informed throughout the load 
interconnection process, as described below. 
1. 
Information-Sharing with the CAISO 
DCRT will provide the CAISO with notice of any delays in DCRT’s construction of 
Interconnection Facilities, Network Upgrades, or Distribution Upgrades.66 This notice requirement 
is reasonable because the CAISO, as the entity responsible for the reliable operation of the CAISO-
controlled transmission system and for transmission planning, must be aware of changes in the 
anticipated in-service dates of facilities that will be integrated into the transmission system over 
which the CAISO exercises operational control, so that the CAISO may account for such changes 
in its operational and planning activities. 
DCRT will provide the CAISO with the specifications for the Large Load Interconnection 
Customer’s Interconnection Facilities and Large Load Facility, as well as “as-built” drawings, 
information, and documents for DCRT’s Interconnection Facilities, including appropriate 
drawings and relay diagrams.67  Providing this information ensures that the CAISO has an accurate 
and complete understanding of the physical and protection characteristics of the facilities being 
connected to the CAISO-controlled transmission system, which is essential to the CAISO’s ability 
to model the transmission system accurately and to maintain reliability. 
To the extent applicable, DCRT will provide the CAISO with monthly construction status 
reports regarding the construction and installation of DCRT’s Interconnection Facilities and 
 
65 See OSC Order, at PP 26, 37, 86 n.189. 
66 See DCRT TO Tariff, proposed Appendix IV, Attachment 3, LLIA, at Art. 5.1.1. 
67 See DCRT TO Tariff, proposed Appendix IV, Attachment 3, LLIA, at Arts. 5.9.1-5.9.3, 5.10.

Network Upgrades.68  These reports will include, but are not limited to: (1) progress to date; (2) a 
description of the activities undertaken since the last report; (3) a description of the action items 
for the next reporting period; and (4) the delivery status of equipment ordered.69 Regular, 
structured reporting of this nature will afford the CAISO ongoing visibility into the status of the 
facilities necessary to serve new Large Loads, consistent with the Commission’s expectation in 
the OSC Order that the CAISO and the CAISO PTOs will coordinate with each other, as 
appropriate, to address the concerns the Commission identified.70 
2. 
Coordination with Affected Systems 
In addition to coordination with the CAISO, DCRT’s proposed LLIP and LLIA recognize 
that the interconnection of a Large Load may affect systems other than DCRT’s own and, 
therefore, includes requirements for coordination with, and the provision of certain information to, 
Affected Systems.71  Specifically, DCRT will conduct a Scoping Meeting with the Large Load 
Interconnection Customer, other Affected Systems, and, as applicable, the CAISO.72  The purpose 
of the Scoping Meeting is to discuss alternative interconnection options; to exchange information, 
including any transmission data and earlier study evaluations that would reasonably be expected 
to impact such interconnection options; and to analyze such information.73 Convening all relevant 
parties at the earliest stage of the LLIP will help to identify potential issues before significant study 
resources are expended, promote the efficient selection of Points of Interconnection, and reduce 
the likelihood of later disputes regarding study assumptions—a concern the Commission 
specifically identified in the OSC Order.74 
Further, under DCRT’s proposed LLIP, the Large Load Interconnection Customer will be 
obligated to enter into a construction and reimbursement agreement with the owner of any Affected 
System and/or other affected owners of portions of the CAISO Controlled Grid that govern 
facilities built on the relevant Affected System.75  Those agreements will govern the construction 
of facilities on Affected Systems and address the payments to be made by the Large Load 
Interconnection Customer to the owner of the Affected System and/or other affected owners of 
portions of the CAISO Controlled Grid, as well as any repayment by such owners.76

3. 
Treatment of Co-Located Generation and BTMG 
DCRT’s proposed LLIA addresses the treatment of co-located generation and BTMG.  
Consistent with the CAISO’s existing practice, and with DCRT’s understanding of the CAISO’s 
position, described above, co-located generation will continue to interconnect through the 
CAISO’s GIDAP process pursuant to a Commission-approved Large Generator Interconnection 
Agreement.77 Facility-specific guidelines and procedures governing any net generation delivered 
to the grid from co-located generation will be addressed in Appendix H of the LLIA.78  
DCRT’s proposal treats a Large Load that is co-located with generation, or that is served 
in part by BTMG, as a Large Load for purposes of the LLIP, while leaving the interconnection of 
the associated generating facility to the CAISO, to the extent that the generation exports power to 
the CAISO Controlled Grid.  A Large Load Interconnection Customer must disclose in its Large 
Load Interconnection Application whether co-located generation is present and, if so, whether the 
generation resource will be fully behind-the-meter or may export to the CAISO Controlled Grid, 
the resource type, nameplate capacity, minimum and maximum operating levels, market 
participation status, and associated resource identifiers, together with all information required by 
DCRT and, as applicable, the CAISO in the Large Generator Interconnection Application.79  
DCRT’s Large Load Interconnection studies will then evaluate the specific operating requirements 
of the combined load and generation configuration, including BTMG, to ensure that the 
interconnection can be accommodated reliably.80 Any generating facility that will export energy 
to the CAISO Controlled Grid, however, must interconnect through the CAISO’s GIDAP and 
execute a Commission-accepted LGIA, consistent with the CAISO’s existing practice and with the 
Commission’s recognition that the CAISO Tariff governs the generator interconnection process.81  
This division of labor ensures that the Large Load interconnection and the generator 
interconnection are each studied under the procedures designed for them, without duplicating the 
CAISO’s generator interconnection function or creating inconsistent study results.  
To ensure that the two processes operate in tandem, DCRT will coordinate with the CAISO 
at each stage of the DCRT LLIP: DCRT will provide to the CAISO a copy of the Large Load 
Interconnection Application, the CAISO may participate with DCRT in designing any FILI 
constraints necessary to maintain reliability, and DCRT will coordinate with the CAISO on testing 
and commissioning of the Large Load and associated interconnection facilities.82  In addition, 
DCRT will coordinate with the CAISO to the extent the interconnection of a Large Load may 
impact the reliability of the CAISO Controlled Grid, and any potential injection of power from a 
Large Load, including from co-located generation, will require such coordination to ensure

compliance with the CAISO Tariff.83  The CAISO’s role further will be memorialized in the DCRT 
LLIA, to which the CAISO will be a party, as stated above, if there is an injection of power to the 
CAISO-controlled grid.  Finally, as noted above, the treatment of any net generation delivered to 
the transmission system from co-located generation or BTMG will be addressed on a project-
specific basis in an appendix to the DCRT LLIA,84 ensuring that each such arrangement is subject 
to clear, enforceable terms that account for its particular operating characteristics.  
DCRT’s proposed approach respects the existing allocation of responsibilities between the 
CAISO, which administers generator interconnection, and the CAISO PTOs, which administer 
load interconnection, while ensuring that the unique operational characteristics of each co-location 
arrangement are captured on a project-specific basis. Indeed, the Commission expressly 
recognized in the OSC Order that the CAISO PTOs’ role in conducting load studies may stem 
from how the CAISO and the CAISO PTOs allocated rights and responsibilities in the governing 
documents establishing the CAISO and stated that it does not seek to alter that allocation.85  
D. 
DCRT’s Proposed Flexible Interim Load Interconnection Service 
As discussed above, DCRT understands that the CAISO has declined to propose a new 
transmission service at this time in light of the CAISO’s day-ahead scheduling process for 
allocating transmission capacity,86 and DCRT defers to the CAISO on this issue. As the 
Commission itself recognized in the OSC Order, the framework under which the CAISO provides 
transmission service is unique among the RTOs/ISOs:  the CAISO does not offer traditional Order 
No. 888 network and point-to-point transmission services, offers no firm, long-term transmission 
reservations of capacity, and instead offers a single “daily” transmission service that is available 
to all Eligible Customers on a day-to-day basis.87   
Rather than proposing a new transmission service, and as envisioned for CAISO PTO’s 
through the CAISO coordination process, DCRT is proposing a new FILI Service that will be 
available when a Large Load’s full requested capacity cannot immediately be provided on a firm 
basis.88  In alignment with DCRT’s understanding of the CAISO’s conception of the FILI Service, 
DCRT’s proposed revisions address: (1) the allocation of responsibility among the relevant parties; 
(2) information-sharing with the CAISO; (3) operational requirements, including curtailment 
mechanisms and data reporting; (4) cost-responsibility provisions; and (5) the transition to firm

service upon completion of the necessary Network Upgrades.  Each of these elements is discussed 
further below. 
1. 
DCRT’s Proposed FILI Service Responds to the Commission’s 
Directives Regarding Flexible Large Loads 
In the OSC Order, the Commission preliminarily found that the CAISO Tariff appears to 
be unjust and unreasonable because it does not include transmission services that reflect Eligible 
Customers taking transmission service on behalf of flexible Large Loads that are willing and able 
to limit their use of the transmission system under certain conditions.89  The Commission directed 
the CAISO to explain whether its Tariff remains just and reasonable without: (1) an interim non-
firm network transmission service while Network Upgrades are being constructed; and (2) 
permanent firm and non-firm contract demand transmission services.90  In the alternative, the 
Commission provided that the CAISO may explain whether, given that the CAISO does not offer 
the transmission services required by Order No. 888, its existing transmission service framework 
addresses the Commission’s concerns, or explain what specific changes to that framework would 
effectively provide comparable transmission service options for flexible Large Loads as in the 
PJM Interconnection, LLC  Co-Location Order.91  The Commission further noted that it has found 
it just and reasonable that, in order to take such interim or contract demand transmission services, 
an Eligible Customer must have necessary control technologies and/or protection systems, which 
may include a special protection scheme, to limit its energy withdrawals to its approved level.92  
In reaching these preliminary findings, the Commission recognized that load flexibility can avoid 
inefficient and costly transmission system build-out and can help timely interconnect flexible 
Large Loads.93  Although these particular directives were addressed to the CAISO, DCRT’s 
proposed FILI Service provides a mechanism for Large Load interconnection to DCRT’s system 
that is consistent with the interim, flexible service the Commission contemplated. 
2. 
Terms and Conditions of FILI Service 
Under Section 3.1 of DCRT’s proposed LLIP, DCRT may offer FILI Service if the System 
Impact Study shows that the Large Load “cannot be reliably served at its full requested demand 
until certain Network Upgrades are completed.”94  In that instance, the Large Load Interconnection 
Customer may request that DCRT offer FILI Service, and DCRT “may propose a customized plan 
. . . based on the type of constraints required to maintain Transmission System reliability.”95 The 
Large Load Interconnection Customer “may participate in the design of the FILI Service 
 
89 See OSC Order, at P 104.

constraints.”96  Once the necessary upgrades are complete, the Large Load Interconnection 
Customer “may elect to receive firm service.”97  This provision serves as the operative trigger for 
FILI Service and provides the pathway for transition to firm service that the OSC Order 
contemplates.98  By allowing the specific constraints of FILI Service to be tailored, with the 
participation of the Large Load Interconnection Customer, to the reliability needs identified in the 
System Impact Study, this approach ensures that FILI Service reasonably limits a Large Load’s 
use of the system only to the extent necessary to maintain reliability on DCRT’s single-line system 
and the CAISO Controlled Grid.  
Under Section A-2 of Appendix A to the LLIA, a Large Load Interconnection Customer 
may elect to take FILI Service until the Network Upgrades identified in Appendix A have achieved 
Commercial Operation.99  This structure tracks the Commission’s description of an interim service 
available “while network upgrades are being constructed.”100  As with the interim service the 
Commission described, DCRT’s proposed FILI Service will allow a flexible Large Load to obtain 
interconnection service more quickly than it otherwise could, in exchange for its willingness to 
accept limitations on its use of the system until the necessary upgrades are complete.101  
Section A-2 of Appendix A to the LLIA, items 2(a)–(g), detail what should be included in 
a project-specific FILI addendum to a LLIA that implements the following concepts, in furtherance 
of the Commission’s directives: (i) study methods revised “to recognize the unique operational 
characteristics of flexible large loads”; (ii) “curtailment implementation mechanisms and data 
reporting”; (iii) financial security “during the conditional service period”; (iv) “automatic 
conversion to firm service upon Network Upgrade completion”; (v) coordination with the study 
process; and (vi) provisions governing longer-duration curtailments “in coordination with, as 
applicable, the CAISO.”102  
Memorializing these terms in an addendum to the LLIA will ensure that the terms and 
conditions of FILI Service are transparent, are applied on a consistent and non-discriminatory basis 
to all similarly situated customers, and are enforceable.103  The curtailment implementation 
mechanisms, in particular, serve the same function as the control technologies and/or protection 
systems that the Commission has found necessary to limit an Eligible Customer’s energy 
withdrawals to its approved level.104

E. 
Cost Responsibility for Network Upgrades 
DCRT understands the concerns about fair and equitable cost responsibility for Large 
Loads and has proposed a mechanism that DCRT believes balances its obligations under the FPA 
and the OSC Order, given that DCRT is a single-asset Transco and does not have a traditional 
integrated transmission system. Specifically, with respect to cost responsibility, Article 11.3.1 of 
the LLIA provides that Large Load Facilities will be reimbursed for Reimbursable Network 
Upgrades costing more than $10 million if the Large Load Facility reaches commercial 
operation.105 DCRT has provided a mechanism pursuant to which DCRT can seek a return of any 
amounts paid to the Large Load Interconnection Customer, to the extent that DCRT is not able to 
recover amounts paid for Reimbursable Network Upgrades in its transmission revenue 
requirement. The claw back period is five (5) years.   
DCRT has proposed this mechanism because of concerns that Network Upgrades required 
for the interconnection of Large Load Facilities may be of a similar scope and size as those required 
under the CAISO generator interconnection process and it may be unduly discriminatory for 
DCRT to maintain different reimbursement procedures for the similar Network Upgrades, 
particularly for a single-asset Transco where the upgrades may be at or near each other.  
Reimbursement of Network Upgrades was a debated issue in the CAISO stakeholder process and 
DCRT understands that its reimbursement policies may be subject to additional discussion as part 
of the CAISO stakeholder process.  
F. 
CAISO Data Exchange 
The Commission directed the CAISO and the PTOs to jointly address provision of data on 
the CAISO’s website in a single location, and in an easily accessible format that is searchable and 
allows users to filter the data, on transmission service provided to Eligible Customers on behalf of 
Large Loads, for Network Upgrades evaluated in the local transmission planning process that are 
needed to accommodate transmission service to Eligible Customers on behalf of those Large 
Loads, and the costs for those Network Upgrades.106  DCRT will coordinate with the CAISO and 
provide all data requested by the CAISO to support the CAISO’s provision of this information in 
the manner directed by the OSC.107   
 
105 See DCRT TO Tariff, proposed Appendix IV, Attachment 3, LLIA, at Art. 11.3.1. 
106 See OSC Order, at P 66. 
107 DCRT will coordinate with CAISO concerning CAISO’s and DCRT’s data exchange and posting obligations and 
will incorporate the details of this process in DCRT’s Large Load interconnection business practices.
